Let me closely study the definitions.

Language:
- labels Low, High; flows_to l1 l2 = if High -> Low then False else True.
- join: Low joined with Low = Low, else High.
- security record with reader, indirect_reader.
- wf_security kappa := flows_to indirect_reader reader.
- security_le kappa1 kappa2 := flows_to reader1 reader2 /\ flows_to indirect1 indirect2.
- public, secret.
- protect_security kappa l := reader := join reader l; indirect_reader := join indirect l.
- ty: Unit kappa, Sum T1 T2 kappa, Prod, Arrow.
- security_of extracts kappa.
- ty_protect l T changes top-level security.
- wf_ty: recursive with kappa wf.
- tm: bvar, fvar, unit, abs, app with label, pair, fst, snd, inl, inr, case with label, protect, if with label.
- open_rec, open, subst, fv, lc_at, locally_closed, closed.
- value: unit, abs (lc), pair, inl, inr.
- protect_value.
- context, lookup, update, context_wf.
- erase_type, erase_security.
- step: beta app requires flows_to kappa.reader r; result tm_protect kappa.indirect_reader (open body v). Similarly fst/snd, case, if.
- multi.
- subtype: reflexive-ish structural with security_le; trans.
- has_type: T_Var, T_Unit, T_Abs, T_App (result ty_protect kappa.indirect_reader T2, requires flows_to kappa.reader r), T_Pair, T_Fst (ty_protect kappa.indirect_reader T1, requires flows_to kappa.reader r), T_Snd similarly, T_Inl/T_Inr, T_Case (ty_protect kappa.indirect_reader T), T_Protect, T_Sub, T_If (condition type Ty_Sum (Ty_Unit public) (Ty_Unit public) k; result ty_protect k.indirect_reader T, requires flows_to k.reader r).
- program_context and plug.
- context_has_type C T_hole T_result := exists L, forall x not in L, has_type (update empty x T_hole) (plug C (tm_fvar x)) T_result.
- ground: Unit true; Sum/Prod conjunction; Arrow false.
- transparent_at kappa T := security_le (security_of T) kappa /\ recursive.
- transparent T := transparent_at (security_of T) T.
- same_result t1 t2 := forall v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> erase_security v1 = erase_security v2.

Target theorem: given C, t1, t2 : tm, T_secret, T_result. has_type empty t1 T_secret; same for t2; context_has_type C T_secret T_result; ground T_result; transparent T_result; ~ flows_to (security_of T_secret).indirect_reader (security_of T_result).indirect_reader; then same_result (plug C t1) (plug C t2).

Perhaps I should just engineer the proof myself. Let me think about what relation makes the fundamental theorem work, and how to handle the arbitrary t1/t2 at T_secret.

Key insight: We don't need a relation that relates t1 and t2 semantically at T_secret if T_secret's indirect reader can't flow to the result's indirect reader. Instead, we can define the relation parameterized by a level ℓ (the "indirect reader" of the result type, or an observer level), such that:
- At level Low (or level ℓ), values at types with security_of T's indirect_reader not flowing to ℓ are unconstrained.
Hmm.

Let's define: R_ℓ(T, v1, v2) := erase_security v1 = erase_security v2, for types T where flows_to (security_of T).(indirect_reader) ℓ? Wait the direction: we need at T_secret (indirect High) with ℓ = Low: High not flows to Low, so no constraint. At Low components with ℓ=Low: Low flows to Low, so constraint erasure equality. That matches intuition: information at level that can flow to observer ℓ must be equal; otherwise unconstrained.

But careful: the level to compare against is the *indirect_reader*, and the elimination rules produce ty_protect kappa.indirect_reader. The protection raises the security level of the result; then the relation at protected type should be unconstrained if High not ≤ Low.

Let's try to define a value relation R_ℓ(T, v1, v2) for values v1, v2 : tm, where ℓ : label is the observer's level (the indirect_reader of the result type? or maybe the reader?). Something like:

R_ℓ(T, v1, v2) := 
  (flows_to (security_of T).(indirect_reader) ℓ -> erase_security v1 = erase_security v2)
  ... plus structural conditions for function types?

Hmm, but for function types we need a Kripke-style relation: functions are related if applying them to related arguments yields related results. However, ground types exclude arrows, and the result type is ground. So kappa.indirect_reader must be Low. Hmm interesting: then T_secret = Arrow T1 T2 Low? Wait the hypothesis concerns T_secret's indirect reader! If T_secret is an arrow with indirect_reader Low, then hypothesis ~ flows_to Low (result indirect) is False since flows_to Low _ = True. So hypothesis fails. Therefore if hypothesis holds, T_secret cannot be used as the function in an application in the context. This is getting complex. Let's think about the actual proof architecture that works.

Approach A: Define a logical relation on *terms* (not just values) with a "level" parameter and prove:
1. Fundamental theorem: if has_type Gamma t T and environments related at Gamma (pointwise R), then R_ℓ(T, t[rho1], t[rho2]).
2. Context lemma: context_has_type C T_hole T_result and R_ℓ(T_hole, t1, t2) implies R_ℓ(T_result, plug C t1, plug C t2).
3. At ground + transparent T_result with ℓ = T_result.indirect_reader... then R implies erase equality of values.

But step 1 with arbitrary t1, t2 of type T_secret requires R_{?}(T_secret, t1, t2) unconditionally, which we only get if the level condition makes it vacuous. So pick ℓ = (security_of T_result).indirect_reader = Low, and T_secret indirect High not ≤ Low, so R should be vacuous at T_secret.

But the fundamental theorem is on *all* types, including arrows. Relation definition must be semantically sensible.

Approach B: Instead of a semantic relation, use a syntactic "erasure equality" argument via a logical relation that directly mirrors typing: define relations on values and terms mutually, with "levels". Let me look at the definitions again for hints about what's expected.

`transparent`: T is transparent if all subcomponents have security ≤ top. `ground`: no arrows. The theorem's conclusion is erase equality of final values. The hypotheses: the secret's *indirect_reader* does not flow to the result's *indirect_reader*. Transparency of result means all subcomponent securities ≤ result security (both reader and indirect). So define V_ℓ(T, v1, v2) with:
```
V_ℓ(T, v1, v2) := 
  if flows_to (security_of T).indirect_reader ℓ then
    match T with
    | Ty_Unit _ => erase_security v1 = erase_security v2   (* trivial *)
    | Ty_Sum T1 T2 _ => (exists w1 w2, v1 = tm_inl w1 _ /\ v2 = tm_inl w2 _ /\ V_ℓ(T1,w1,w2)) \/ (exists ..., inr ...)
    | Ty_Prod T1 T2 _ => exists w1 w2 w1' w2', v1 = tm_pair w1 w2 _ /\ v2 = tm_pair w1' w2' _ /\ V_ℓ(T1,w1,w1') /\ V_ℓ(T2,w2,w2')
    | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa => forall w1 w2, V_ℓ(T1,w1,w2) -> ??? application related
    end
  else True.
```

No external source. We must construct the proof ourselves.

Let me now think carefully and design a full proof. This is a substantial development. Let me plan the architecture in detail, then implement incrementally with compilation.

Key components:

1. Basic label lemmas:
   - flows_to_refl, flows_to_trans, join properties: flows_to l (join l l')? etc.
   - flows_to Low b always True; ~ flows_to High Low.
   - security_le reflexivity/transitivity.
   - `join` monotone: flows_to a a' -> flows_to b b' -> flows_to (join a b) (join a' b').
   - protect_security monotone, security_le protect etc.

2. Type-level lemmas:
   - wf_ty lemmas.
   - `security_le (protect_security kappa l) kappa'` conditions.
   - ty_protect idempotent-ish.
   - erase_type properties maybe.

3. Term-level lemmas:
   - open/subst/fv/lc_at interactions:
     - fv_subst_env, lc_at_subst? 
     - open with values and substitution commuting.
     - erase_security of protect_value = erase_security v; erase_security of tm_protect.
   - value lemmas: values are locally closed; values don't step.
   - step determinism.
   - evaluation of protect: tm_protect l t -->* v -> exists w, t -->* w /\ value w /\ v = protect_value w l.
   - multi lemmas: transitivity; app congruence (if t1 -->* t1' then app t1 t2 r -->* app t1' t2 r); ST_AppAbs as multi; beta.
   - maybe "step is deterministic" to help same_result from two evaluations? Not needed if R gives both.

4. Substitution/environment typing lemma:
   - Need `has_type` preserved under substitution of terms for free atoms? Actually for the fundamental theorem via env substitution, our IH handles the open body case. But T_Abs's premise is has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open body (fvar x)) T2 for x fresh. We'll apply IH directly with that. The environment relation at x uses w1,w2 values. So no general typing substitution lemma needed! Wait: IH statement is for arbitrary term `t` and env sigma; in T_Abs we take t := open body (fvar x), which is arbitrary syntax. So no substitution lemma needed. But then the conclusion of IH is R at T2 for `subst_env sigma1' (open body (fvar x))`. We connect to `open (subst_env sigma1 body) w1` by the open/subst commutation lemma. So we need only syntax lemmas, not typing substitution. 

   However, we must choose x fresh not just from L but also such that the equality holds. The T_Abs premise gives the property for all x ∉ L. We need additionally x ∉ fv body (which is finite). Choose x' = fresh (L ++ fv body). Since the premise holds for all x not in L, and x' not in L, it holds. And x' ∉ fv body. Good.
   Similarly for T_Case: bodies body1/body2 opened with fresh x; choose x not in L, fv body1, fv body2, and also not in whatever. Fine.

   But also, for the env extension, sigma' x = w1; we need R at T1 for w1,w2 which we have as V.

5. The logical relation definitions.

Let me settle the definitions precisely.

Parameter ℓ : label (observer level). Define:

```
Fixpoint V (l : label) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit kappa => True   (* maybe erase equality, equivalent *)
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa =>
      if flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l then
        (exists w1 w2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inl w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl w2 k2 /\ V l T1 w1 w2)
        \/ (exists w1 w2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inr w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inr w2 k2 /\ V l T2 w1 w2)
      else True
  ...
```

Wait, should the vacuity check be on kappa.indirect_reader of the type, or on something else? Let's think again about the semantics of the relation. The key property we need at the end: for ground transparent T_result and ℓ = security_of T_result.indirect_reader, V ℓ T_result v1 v2 -> erase v1 = erase v2. With transparent, every subcomponent U has indirect ≤ result.indirect = ℓ, so the non-vacuous branch is taken everywhere. With ground, no arrows. Then structural equality implies erase equality. Good.

The key property at T_secret: ℓ = result indirect Low, and ~ flows_to T_secret.indirect ℓ, so V ℓ T_secret = True. Hence R holds for any terms. Good.

The fundamental theorem must show: for all rules, if has_type Gamma t T then R ℓ T (subst sigma1 t) (subst sigma2 t) given env relation.

Let's check each typing rule for provability. The relation R is:

```
Definition R (l : label) (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 -> value v1 -> t2 -->* v2 -> value v2 -> V l T v1 v2.
```

Actually `evaluates t v := t -->* v /\ value v`. So R := forall v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> V l T v1 v2.

Now, typing rules:

T_Var: env gives R at T. ✓.

T_Unit: t = tm_unit kappa, type Ty_Unit kappa. V = True. ✓.

T_Abs: discussed. Need V at Arrow. Let's define V at arrow:

```
| Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa =>
    if flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l then
      forall (w1 w2 : tm) (r : label),
        V l T1 w1 w2 ->
        flows_to kappa.(reader) r ->
        R l (ty_protect kappa.(indirect_reader) T2) (tm_app v1 w1 r) (tm_app v2 w2 r)
    else True
```

Hmm, wait: should the level ℓ for the argument V be ℓ, or should the arrow relation be more subtle? Consider a function of type Arrow T1 T2 kappa where kappa.indirect_reader is High not ≤ ℓ: vacuous, so the function can leak information about its argument into its result? If the result is protected at High, the result type is ty_protect High T2, whose indirect is High, so at level ℓ it's vacuous. So yes, any function behavior is fine. If kappa.indirect_reader ≤ ℓ, then the function's results at T2 (protected) are observable at ℓ; but wait, the protection is join kappa.indirect T2's own security. If kappa.indirect ≤ ℓ, then observable. The *argument* T1 might itself be secret (High) relative to ℓ; then V at T1 is vacuous, so the function may be applied to arbitrary values and its results must still be related at T2 protected. Is that semantically right? If the function type is public (indirect Low) but its argument type is secret (indirect High), then the function is assumed not to leak the argument: but our relation says for *any* pair of values at T1 (vacuous relation), the applications must produce related results. Since V at T1 is True for any values w1, w2 (regardless of behavior), the relation demands the function produce equal-erasure results for arbitrary secret arguments. That's the correct parametricity/noninterference condition for a public function taking secrets. But is it *provable* from typing? Suppose the function is tm_abs T1 body kappa with body returning a constant public unit. Then yes. If the body branches on the secret argument and returns different public results, typing would require elimination of T1 and produce protected results at High, which can't be subtyped to Low T2 — unless T1 is a unit type (no elimination) or arrow (application with result protected at High). So typing enforces it. Good.

But what about the level for the argument relation: should V at T1 be with level ℓ or with kappa.reader? Hmm. The context using the function may pass arguments of type T1 that are themselves observed at ℓ. The function's type has reader kappa.reader; the application requires flows_to kappa.reader r. If kappa.reader is High while ℓ is Low, then an application with r=Low is not allowed (flows_to High Low false), but r=High is allowed. Hmm, the label r does not affect the result dynamics. The typing of the application at label r: flows_to kappa.reader r. The result type is protected at kappa.indirect. The observer ℓ sees the result if kappa.indirect ≤ ℓ. But if kappa.reader = High and ℓ = Low, can the function's result at Low be observed? Yes, if kappa.indirect = Low. Wait, can wf_security kappa have reader High, indirect Low? flows_to Low High = True, yes. So a function whose reader is High but indirect Low: eliminating it releases Low info. Our V at arrow with the test on indirect Low ≤ ℓ=Low is non-vacuous, requiring related results for related args at V_ℓ(T1). Seems right.

But is the argument relation at level ℓ correct? Or should it be at level r? Let's think about the semantics of reader/indirect_reader again: I suspect this type system is designed so that the *reader* level indicates the confidentiality of the value, and *indirect_reader* indicates the level at which the *result of eliminating* the value is protected. Actually maybe reader = level of direct observation allowed, indirect_reader = level of indirect observation allowed. The typing rule for app: flows_to kappa.reader r where r is the level of the application context? Hmm, but r is discarded. Actually maybe r represents the "indirect reader" of the evaluation context, i.e., who observes the evaluation of this computation? The rule says the function's reader must flow to r, meaning r is allowed to read the function. And the result is protected at kappa.indirect_reader, meaning the result inherits the function's indirect level. Hmm, if r were the observer level, then the result protection should be join kappa.indirect_reader r? But it's just kappa.indirect_reader. In the target theorem, the relation is between secret.indirect and result.indirect, not about r's. So I'll go with our design.

Let's sanity check the theorem statement's secret assumption: T_secret has indirect High (since hypothesis satisfiable). Values of type T_secret related vacuously at ℓ=Low. But wait: the context C may *return* a value of type T_result derived from eliminating t. But could the context pass t as an argument to a public function whose argument type is T_secret and whose result is public? Conclusion: R ℓ T_result (subst sigma1 (plug C (fvar x))) (subst sigma2 ...) = R ℓ T_result (plug C t1) (plug C t2) (need subst fvar lemma). Then same_result follows from R + V->erase for ground transparent T_result.

Now the meat: fundamental theorem. Let's carefully check each case and identify necessary lemmas.

The theorem statement with environment:

```
Definition env_rel (l : label) (Gamma : context) (s1 s2 : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, lookup_context x Gamma = Some T -> R l T (s1 x) (s2 x).
```

Fundamental theorem:
```
Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall l s1 s2, env_rel l Gamma s1 s2 ->
  R l T (subst_env s1 t) (subst_env s2 t).
```

Proposed definition:

```
V l (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa) v1 v2 :=
  match v1, v2 with
  | tm_abs _ b1 k1, tm_abs _ b2 k2 =>
      wf_security k1 /\ wf_security k2 /\   (* maybe not needed *)

      (if flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l then
         forall w1 w2 r,
           V l T1 w1 w2 ->
           flows_to k1.(reader) r -> flows_to k2.(reader) r ->
           R l (ty_protect k1.(indirect_reader) T2) (tm_app v1 w1 r) ... 
      ...
  | _, _ => True
  end
```

This suggests defining V at Arrow by matching the values and using the *values'* annotations for application semantics, while returning a relation at the type's declared protection:

```
V l (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa) v1 v2 :=
  match v1, v2 with
  | tm_abs _ b1 k1, tm_abs _ b2 k2 =>
      security_le k1 kappa /\ security_le k2 kappa /\
      (if flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l then
         forall w1 w2 r, V l T1 w1 w2 ->
           flows_to k1.(reader) r -> flows_to k2.(reader) r ->
           R l (ty_protect kappa.(indirect_reader) T2) (tm_app v1 w1 r) (tm_app v2 w2 r)
       else True)
  | _, _ => True  (* or True regardless *)
  end

Since V is defined with an if on flows_to, we should write the definition so that proofs can case on `flows_to ...` explicitly. In Coq, `if flows_to a l then X else True` where flows_to is a Prop; `if` on a Prop requires a decision? No—`flows_to` returns Prop, and `if` requires a Boolean (unless using `match` on a proof? Coq's `if b then ... else ...` requires b : bool). Wait, `flows_to l1 l2 : Prop` is defined as a match returning Prop: `match l1,l2 with High,Low => False | _, _ => True end`. You cannot `if` on a Prop directly. In the task's definitions, they use `match l1, l2 with ... end` to return Prop. For V, I need to write a match returning Prop, i.e.:

```
Definition V (l : label) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa =>
      match flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l with
      ...
```
Since flows_to is a Prop, we can't match on it to decide; but we can write the Prop as `flows_to kappa.ind l -> ...`? Hmm, the vacuity is: if not flows_to, then True. That's equivalent to `flows_to kappa.ind l -> Structural`. Because when flows_to is False, the implication is vacuously True. When True, requires Structural. So we can define V using implication instead of if:

```
V l T v1 v2 :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa =>
      flows_to kappa.ind l -> 
        (exists w1 w2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inl w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl w2 k2 /\ V l T1 w1 w2)
        \/ (... inr ...)
  ...
```
This is logically equivalent to the if-form when flows_to is decidable (it is: match on labels), and more convenient for proofs? For case analysis, we can destruct (flows_to ...) as a Prop? Not directly, but flows_to is a match on labels, so destructing kappa.ind and l reduces it to True or False. We can prove a case lemma: flows_to l1 l2 is either True or False, and specifically if ~flows_to then... Use `destruct l1, l2; simpl`. To destruct a hypothesis h : flows_to a b, `destruct a, b` works.

For proving the target's implication form from premise implication form: when target's antecedent (join(ind,b) ≤ l) holds, derive premise's antecedent (ind ≤ l) by transitivity, apply premise, get structure, then use it to prove target structure. The target structure is the same structural relation but with the *values* v1,v2 (or protect_value...)—for (P) they're protected. Works.

But for using V in proofs, the implication form is nice: to apply V at a non-vacuous level, provide the flows_to proof; to prove V when vacuous, introduce the flows_to hypothesis and derive contradiction? Hmm, but wait: with implication form, V at a type whose indirect doesn't flow to l is NOT definitionally True, it's `flows_to ... -> Structural`. It's logically True (since the premise is False), but not definitionally. That's okay; we can prove it from `~flows_to`.

Let me define:
```
Fixpoint V (l : label) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa =>
      flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l ->
      ( (exists w1 w2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inl w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl w2 k2 /\ V l T1 w1 w2)
        \/ (exists w1 w2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inr w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inr w2 k2 /\ V l T2 w1 w2) )
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 kappa =>
      flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l ->
      (exists w1 w2 w1' w2' k1 k2, v1 = tm_pair w1 w2 k1 /\ v2 = tm_pair w1' w2' k2 /\ V l T1 w1 w1' /\ V l T2 w2 w2')
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa =>
      flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l ->
      match v1, v2 with
      | tm_abs _ b1 k1, tm_abs _ b2 k2 =>
          security_le k1 kappa /\ security_le k2 kappa /\
          (forall w1 w2, V l T1 w1 w2 -> R l T2 (open b1 w1) (open b2 w2))
      | _, _ => True
      end
  end
with R (l : label) (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 -> value v1 -> t2 -->* v2 -> value v2 -> V l T v1 v2.
```

```
Lemma V_subtype : forall T U (H : subtype T U), forall l v1 v2, V l T v1 v2 -> V l U v1 v2.

```

Define `subst_env (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm` structurally (like subst but for all atoms at once):
```
Fixpoint subst_env sigma t :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_unit k => tm_unit k
  | tm_abs T b k => tm_abs T (subst_env sigma b) k
  | tm_app t1 t2 r => tm_app (subst_env sigma t1) (subst_env sigma t2) r
  ...
  | tm_protect l t => tm_protect l (subst_env sigma t)
  | tm_if c y n r => tm_if (subst_env sigma c) (subst_env sigma y) (subst_env sigma n) r
  end.
```

Identity:
```
Lemma subst_env_open : forall sigma w x body,
  (forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)) ->
  locally_closed w ->
  ~ In x (fv body) ->
  subst_env (fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then w else sigma y) (open body (tm_fvar x))
  = open (subst_env sigma body) w.
```

```
Lemma subst_env_open_rec : forall sigma w x body k,
  (forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)) ->
  locally_closed w ->
  ~ In x (fv body) ->
  subst_env (fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then w else sigma y) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) body)

```

Now, crucially: the fundamental theorem's IH is on the typing derivation and the env. Now let's reconsider the definition of env_rel:
```
Definition env_rel (l : label) (Gamma : context) (s1 s2 : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  (forall y, locally_closed (s1 y) /\ locally_closed (s2 y)) /\
  (forall x T, lookup_context x Gamma = Some T -> R l T (s1 x) (s2 x)).
```
In T_Abs, extending s_i with x↦w_i: lc preserved; R at x: R_of_V_value. For y≠x: lookup update = lookup Gamma (need eqb false); R from env_rel. ✓.

Lemma V_protect_adjust : forall T l v1 v2,

Now, what about the case where V at Prod's disjunction requires the pair to match; multi_fst_inv gives runtime pair values; the pair value from R at Prod is exactly that value; constructor injectivity. ✓.

Now the `same_result` final lemma and the theorem:

```
Theorem noninterference ... 
Proof.
  intros C t1 t2 T_secret T_result Ht1 Ht2 HC Hg Htr Hnf.
  destruct HC as [L HC].
  set (x0 := fresh (L ++ fv (plug C (tm_fvar 0)))).
  assert (HxL : ~ In x0 L) by (apply fresh_notin; apply in_or_app; left; ...).
  assert (HxC : ~ In x0 (fv (plug C (tm_fvar 0)))) by (apply fresh_notin; apply in_or_app; right).
  assert (Htyp : has_type (update empty x0 T_secret) (plug C (tm_fvar x0)) T_result) by (apply HC; exact HxL).
  assert (Hlc1 : locally_closed t1) by apply has_type_lc in Ht1.
  assert (Hlc2 : locally_closed t2).
  set (s1 := fun y => if Nat.eqb y x0 then t1 else tm_fvar y).
  set (s2 := ... t2).
  assert (Henv : env_rel (security_of T_result).(indirect_reader) (update empty x0 T_secret) s1 s2).
  { split.
    - intros y; split; destruct (Nat.eqb y x0) eqn:E; [rewrite -> t1 ... lc | apply lc_at fvar] }
    - intros y T Hy. unfold update in Hy. simpl in Hy.
      (* lookup y [x0:T_secret] = if eqb y x0 then Some T_secret else None *)

        unfold R; intros v1 v2 [ev1 hv1] [ev2 hv2].
        (* V at T_secret is vacuous *)
        unfold V. destruct T_secret; simpl.
        ...
      + (* lookup = None, contradiction *) discriminate Hy.
  }
  pose proof (fundamental ... Htyp ... Henv) as HR.
  (* HR : R ℓ T_result (subst_env s1 (plug C (fvar x0))) (subst_env s2 ...) *)
  rewrite (subst_env_single_plug ...) in HR; [| exact HxC]...

Hmm, the subst identity: with s1 defined as if-eqb to t1 else fvar. `subst_env s1 (plug C (tm_fvar x0)) = plug C t1` given x0 doesn't occur in C's fixed parts. We need HxC: x0 ∉ fv (plug C (tm_fvar 0)). But does that imply x0 doesn't occur in C's fixed parts? fv (plug C (fvar 0)) = fv(C's fixed parts) ++ [0]. If x0 ∉ that list and x0 ≠ 0, then x0 not in fv(C's fixed parts). ✓. And the identity lemma can be stated as:
```
Lemma subst_env_plug : forall sigma C x,
  ~ In x (fv (plug C (tm_fvar 0))) ->
  subst_env sigma (plug C (tm_fvar x)) = plug C (sigma x).
```
But wait: this identity requires that for atoms y ≠ x occurring in C's fixed parts, sigma y = fvar y. Our s1 satisfies that by definition (if y≠x0 then fvar y). And x0 not in fixed parts ensures the fixed parts don't contain x0 (so sigma x0 = t1 doesn't affect them; sigma y = fvar y for y≠x0 keeps them). But in the induction, when we encounter the hole (fvar x0), sigma x0 = t1; when we encounter fixed fvar y, y ≠ x0 (since x0 not in fv of fixed parts), sigma y = fvar y. So the identity holds for *any* sigma with that property. Let's state and prove:
```
Lemma subst_env_plug : forall sigma C x,
  (forall y, y <> x -> sigma y = tm_fvar y) ->
  ~ In x (fv (plug C (tm_fvar 0))) ->
  subst_env sigma (plug C (tm_fvar x)) = plug C (sigma x).
```
Hmm, the hypothesis `forall y, y<>x -> sigma y = fvar y` is exactly what s1/s2 satisfy? s1 y = if eqb y x0 then t1 else fvar y; for y≠x0, s1 y = fvar y requires eqb y x0 = false, which follows from y≠x0 via Nat.eqb_neq. ✓. Also for y = x0, no constraint.

But actually the condition needed is only for y occurring in the fixed parts of C; since x0 is fresh from all of them, any y in fixed parts is ≠ x0, so sigma y = fvar y. The proof by induction on C: at a node, e.g., C_AppLeft C' t2 r: subst_env sigma (plug C) = app (subst_env sigma (plug C' (fvar x))) (subst_env sigma t2) r. We need subst_env sigma t2 = t2. t2 is a fixed part; every atom y in fv t2 is in fv (plug C (fvar 0)) hence y ≠ x0; and sigma y = fvar y by hypothesis. So subst_env sigma t2 = t2 (needs lemma `subst_env_id` : (forall y, In y (fv t) -> sigma y = fvar y) -> subst_env sigma t = t`). And IH applies if ~ In x (fv (plug C' (fvar 0))) — from the hypothesis and fv decomposition. ✓.

Actually, maybe we can avoid induction on C for the subst identity by a general lemma `subst_env_plug`: subst_env sigma (plug C t) = plug C (subst_env sigma t) for ANY sigma and t? Let's check: plug C t has holes at positions of t; subst_env distributes over plug structurally, and the fixed parts of C are also substituted by sigma (sv). Because plug C is a syntactic context with a hole; substituting free atoms structurally commutes with plugging: both are structural substitutions. These differ by subst t2! So the identity holds only if subst t2 = t2. Right, because plug C (subst t) does not substitute the fixed parts, whereas subst_env (plug C t) does. Now, the V function definition in Coq. It's a Fixpoint on T returning Prop, with R defined after? V references R, and R references V. Mutual recursion? R is defined via multi and V, V references R in the arrow case. So we need a mutual Fixpoint:

```
Fixpoint V (l : label) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa => ...
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 kappa => ...
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa =>
      flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l ->
      match v1, v2 with
      | tm_abs _ b1 k1, tm_abs _ b2 k2 =>
          security_le k1 kappa /\ security_le k2 kappa /\
          (forall w1 w2, value w1 -> value w2 -> V l T1 w1 w2 ->
             R l T2 (open b1 w1) (open b2 w2))
      | _, _ => True
      end
  end
with R (l : label) (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> V l T v1 v2.
```
Can a mutual Fixpoint have R structurally decreasing on T while calling V? R's body calls V l T ... with the same T; V decreases on subterms. The mutual fixpoint must satisfy the guard condition: in R's body, V is called with the same T, which is not a subterm of T? In mutual recursion, calls between the functions must be on the same or smaller arguments according to the recursion principle. Since R and V both take T as the decreasing argument, R calling V with the same T should be allowed if the mutual fixpoint's guard accepts it (same argument = not greater). I believe Coq's guard checker allows mutual calls with the same decreasing argument (V is defined "before" R? In a mutual block, R's call to V with T equal to R's decreasing argument is fine). But V calls R in the arrow case with T2, a subterm of Arrow T1 T2 kappa; R is in the block with the same decreasing argument T, so the call R l T2 ... should be on a smaller argument ✓ (T2 is a subterm). Hmm, but mutual fixpoints in Coq require the principal argument to be the same for all functions and the recursive calls to be on subterms with the same principal argument position. The call from V to R has decreasing arg T2 (smaller than T) and the call from R to V has T (equal). I think the guard checker accepts "equal" calls in mutual recursion? Not sure. To be safe, avoid mutual recursion: define V as a Fixpoint that takes a *relation* as a parameter? Or define R after V, and in V's arrow case use a parameter relation for the recursive result.

Trick: define V parameterized by `Trel : label -> ty -> tm -> tm -> Prop` (the term relation), and instantiate with a Fixpoint-like definition? But then V's arrow case uses Trel at T2; we want Trel = R = (fun l T t1 t2 => forall v1 v2, evaluates... V l T v1 v2). We can define V0 (Rparam) and then define V as a Fixpoint where the arrow case calls Rparam... but Rparam would be R which is defined after V and refers back. Standard solution: define a single Fixpoint taking the term relation as a *parameter* where the arrow case uses the parameter at T2, and then instantiate by a fixed point? Hmm.

Alternative: define V and R with R inside V as a local definition? Or define V as a Fixpoint with the arrow case containing `forall v1' v2', evaluates (open b1 w1) v1' -> evaluates (open b2 w2) v2' -> V l T2 v1' v2'` — i.e., inline R's definition. Then R is a separate Definition that calls V; but V's arrow case would need the "evaluates ... -> V l T2" inlined, which is exactly R at T2, but inlined without referencing R. Since R is just notation for that forall, we can inline in V:

```
Fixpoint V l T v1 v2 :=
  match T with
  ...
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa =>
      flows_to kappa.ind l ->
      match v1,v2 with
      | tm_abs _ b1 k1, tm_abs _ b2 k2 =>
          security_le k1 kappa /\ security_le k2 kappa /\
          (forall w1 w2, value w1 -> value w2 -> V l T1 w1 w2 ->
             forall z1 z2, evaluates (open b1 w1) z1 -> evaluates (open b2 w2) z2 -> V l T2 z1 z2)
      | _,_ => True end
  end.

Definition R l T t1 t2 := forall v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> V l T v1 v2.
```
Is the Fixpoint V structurally recursive? The arrow case calls V l T1 (subterm) and V l T2 (subterm). ✓ No mutual recursion needed. And in proofs, when we need R at T2 inside the arrow case, we can `change` the inlined forall to R l T2 (open b1 w1) (open b2 w2)`. Since R is a Definition, `change (R l T2 ...)` should work or `unfold R`. The inlined form is definitionally equal to R. ✓.

Great, this avoids mutual recursion issues.

Note: In V's arrow case, `forall z1 z2, evaluates ... -> ... -> V l T2 z1 z2` is exactly R l T2 (open b1 w1) (open b2 w2) by unfolding R. In proofs, `simpl` might unfold both V and R... careful, but manageable.

Also, V at Sum/Prod: use existential with annotations and security_le. Let me write the definitions:

```
Fixpoint V (l : label) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa =>
      flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l ->
      ( (exists (w1 w2 : tm) (k1 k2 : security),
            v1 = tm_inl w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl w2 k2 /\
            security_le k1 kappa /\ security_le k2 kappa /\
            V l T1 w1 w2)
        \/ (exists (w1 w2 : tm) (k1 k2 : security),
            v1 = tm_inr w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inr w2 k2 /\
            security_le k1 kappa /\ security_le k2 kappa /\
            V l T2 w1 w2) )
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 kappa =>
      flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l ->
      (exists (w1 w2 w1' w2' : tm) (k1 k2 : security),
          v1 = tm_pair w1 w2 k1 /\ v2 = tm_pair w1' w2' k2 /\
          security_le k1 kappa /\ security_le k2 kappa /\
          V l T1 w1 w1' /\ V l T2 w2 w2')
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa =>
      flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l ->
      match v1, v2 with
      | tm_abs _ b1 k1, tm_abs _ b2 k2 =>
          security_le k1 kappa /\ security_le k2 kappa /\
          (forall (w1 w2 : tm), value w1 -> value w2 -> V l T1 w1 w2 ->
             R l T2 (open b1 w1) (open b2 w2))
      | _, _ => True
      end
  end
with R ... -- can't, so define R after.
```

Lemma V_subtype : forall T U, subtype T U -> forall l v1 v2, V l T v1 v2 -> V l U v1 v2.

(* ===== Basic label and security lemmas ===== *)

Lemma flows_to_refl : forall l : label, flows_to l l.
Proof. intros []; simpl; trivial. Qed.

Lemma flows_to_trans : forall a b c : label,
  flows_to a b -> flows_to b c -> flows_to a c.
Proof. intros [] [] []; simpl in *; intuition. Qed.

Lemma flows_to_low_l : forall l, flows_to Low l.
Proof. intros []; simpl; trivial. Qed.

Lemma flows_to_high_high : flows_to High High.
Proof. simpl; trivial. Qed.

Lemma not_flows_to_high_low : ~ flows_to High Low.
Proof. simpl; tauto. Qed.

Lemma flows_to_join_l : forall a b, flows_to a (join a b).
Proof. intros [] []; simpl; trivial. Qed.

Lemma flows_to_join_r : forall a b, flows_to b (join a b).
Proof. intros [] []; simpl; trivial. Qed.

Lemma join_mono : forall a b c d,
  flows_to a b -> flows_to c d -> flows_to (join a c) (join b d).
Proof. intros [] [] [] []; simpl in *; intuition. Qed.
```

  security_le k1 k2 -> security_le k2 k3 -> security_le k1 k3.

  intros [r1 i1] [r2 i2] [r3 i3]; simpl.

Lemma open_rec_lc : forall k m t u, k <= m -> lc_at k t -> open_rec m u t = t.

`has_type_lc`. Let me write with explicit induction. We need fresh for L. Let me define fresh first.

Fresh:
```
Fixpoint max_list (xs : list nat) : nat :=
  match xs with
  | [] => 0
  | x :: xs' => Nat.max x (max_list xs')
  end.

Definition fresh (xs : list nat) : nat := S (max_list xs).

Lemma max_list_bound : forall xs x, In x xs -> x <= max_list xs.
Proof.
  induction xs; intros x Hin; simpl in *.
  - contradiction.
  - destruct Hin as [<- | Hin].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + eapply Nat.le_trans; [apply IHxs; exact Hin | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh_notin : forall xs, ~ In (fresh xs) xs.
Proof.
  intros xs Hin. unfold fresh in Hin.
  pose proof (max_list_bound xs (S (max_list xs)) Hin). lia.
Qed.
```
`Nat.le_max_l : forall n m, n <= Nat.max n m`; `Nat.le_max_r`. ✓.

Lemma lc_open_fvar_inv : forall n body x,

Generalized lemma:
```
Lemma subst_env_open_rec : forall s x w body k,
  (forall y, y <> x -> locally_closed (s y)) ->
  ~ In x (fv body) ->
  subst_env (fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then w else s y) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) body)
  = open_rec k (subst_env s body) w.
```
Proof by induction on body with k generalized. But we also need the auxiliary fact `open_rec k (s y) w = s y` for lc s y — this is open_rec_lc0 with m=k: lc_at 0 (s y) → open_rec k w (s y) = s y. ✓.

For the induction, the freshness hypothesis is about the *subterm's* fv: when body = abs T b, fv body = fv b, so ~In x (fv b) ✓. When body = app b1 b2, fv = fv b1 ++ fv b2; need ~In x (fv b1) and ~In x (fv b2): from In x (fv b1) -> In x (fv b1++fv b2) via in_or_app. ✓. Similarly for the others. Let me define subst_env:
```
Fixpoint subst_env (s : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => s x
  | tm_unit kappa => tm_unit kappa
  | tm_abs T body kappa => tm_abs T (subst_env s body) kappa
  | tm_app t1 t2 r => tm_app (subst_env s t1) (subst_env s t2) r
  | tm_pair t1 t2 kappa => tm_pair (subst_env s t1) (subst_env s t2) kappa
  | tm_fst t1 r => tm_fst (subst_env s t1) r
  | tm_snd t1 r => tm_snd (subst_env s t1) r
  | tm_inl t1 kappa => tm_inl (subst_env s t1) kappa
  | tm_inr t1 kappa => tm_inr (subst_env s t1) kappa
  | tm_case t0 b1 b2 r => tm_case (subst_env s t0) (subst_env s b1) (subst_env s b2) r
  | tm_protect l t1 => tm_protect l (subst_env s t1)
  | tm_if c y n r => tm_if (subst_env s c) (subst_env s y) (subst_env s n) r
  end.
```
Note: substitution under binders doesn't need to shift bvars since bvars are not atoms (no capture). ✓.

Next, `ctx_atoms`:
```
Fixpoint ctx_atoms (C : program_context) : list atom :=
  match C with
  | C_Hole => []
  | C_Abs T C' k => ctx_atoms C'
  | C_AppLeft C' t r => ctx_atoms C' ++ fv t
  | C_AppRight t C' r => fv t ++ ctx_atoms C'
  ...
  end.
```

This is exactly a single-atom substitution. We can prove a relation with plug:
For any term u: subst_env s u = subst_atom x t u, where subst_atom is the existing `subst x t u`! Indeed the task defines `subst x u t` structurally replacing fvar x with u. And `fun y => if eqb y x then t else fvar y` corresponds to substitution for atom x.

`subst_env_open_rec` proof: I'll write with as-patterns. The statement:
```
Lemma subst_env_open_rec : forall s x w body k,
  (forall y, y <> x -> locally_closed (s y)) ->
  ~ In x (fv body) ->
  subst_env (fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then w else s y) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) body)
  = open_rec k (subst_env s body) w.
```
Induction on body generalizing k. Let's denote sigma := fun y => if eqb y x then w else s y.

Cases:
- bvar i: LHS = subst_env sigma (if eqb k i then fvar x else bvar i). If eqb k i true: subst_env sigma (fvar x) = sigma x = w; RHS = open_rec k (subst_env s (bvar i)) w = open_rec k (bvar i) w = if eqb k i then w else bvar i = w. ✓. If false: LHS = bvar i; RHS = if false then w else bvar i = bvar i. 
  In Coq: `simpl. destruct (Nat.eqb k i); simpl; [rewrite Nat.eqb_refl; reflexivity | reflexivity].` Hmm: after `simpl`, the LHS's inner open_rec reduces to the if; then subst_env of if? subst_env doesn't reduce an `if` at the head unless we destruct. So `destruct (Nat.eqb k i)`; then simpl; LHS subst_env sigma (fvar x) = sigma x = if eqb x x then w else s x = w; RHS if true then w else bvar i = w. `simpl. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity.`
  Need to unfold sigma: sigma x is `if Nat.eqb x x then w else s x`. simpl reduces eqb x x? `Nat.eqb_refl` or `simpl` may reduce Nat.eqb on variables? eqb x x is not definitionally true for variable x (Nat.eqb is a fixpoint on x, can't reduce). Use `rewrite Nat.eqb_refl`. But the term is `(fun y => if y =? x then w else s y) x` which beta-reduces to `if x =? x then w else s x`. Then rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. ✓.
- fvar y: LHS = sigma y; RHS = open_rec k (s y) w. Need sigma y = s y since y ≠ x (from Hnin: ~In x [y] gives y ≠ x). And open_rec k (s y) w = s y by open_rec_lc0 (Hlc y Hneq). So: `simpl. destruct (Nat.eqb y x) eqn:E. + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; exfalso; apply Hnin; simpl; exact E. + rewrite open_rec_lc0; [reflexivity | apply Hlc; intro Heq; apply Hnin; simpl; exact Heq].` Wait: in the true branch E : (y =? x) = true → y = x; Hnin : ~In x [y] → x ≠ y; contradiction. In the false branch, LHS = s y, RHS = open_rec k (s y) w; use open_rec_lc0 with Hlc y ?neq: need y ≠ x, from E false via Nat.eqb_neq. 
  Note open_rec_lc0 : forall m t u, lc_at 0 t -> open_rec m u t = t. With m=k, t=s y, u=w. ✓.
- unit: LHS = unit; RHS = open_rec k (unit) w = unit (open_rec doesn't touch unit? open_rec k u (tm_unit kappa) = tm_unit kappa ✓). simpl; reflexivity.
- abs T b: LHS = subst_env sigma (tm_abs T (open_rec (S k) (fvar x) b) kappa) = tm_abs T (subst_env sigma (open_rec (S k) ... b)) kappa. RHS = open_rec k (tm_abs T (subst_env s b) kappa) w = tm_abs T (open_rec (S k) (subst_env s b) w) kappa. Apply f_equal; apply IHbody with (k:=S k); Hnin: ~In x (fv b) from Hnin (fv abs = fv b), Hlc same. ✓.
- app: LHS = app (subst_env sigma (open_rec k (fvar x) t1)) (subst_env sigma (open_rec k (fvar x) t2)) r; RHS = app (open_rec k (subst_env s t1) w) (open_rec k (subst_env s t2) w) r. Use IH1, IH2 with k; Hnin components from in_app. ✓.
- pair same.

- if: c,y,n all k. ✓.

For subst_env_plug_hole, with s := fun y => if eqb y x then t else fvar y:
```
Lemma subst_env_plug_hole : forall C x t,
  ~ In x (ctx_atoms C) ->
  subst_env (fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then t else tm_fvar y) (plug C (tm_fvar x)) = plug C t.

This avoids associativity issues because `simpl` normalizes the fv of the case into the same associativity as the definition, and `repeat rewrite in_app_iff` decomposes any nesting; tauto then uses Hin. Let me replace all the `notin_app_*` derivations in subst_env_open_rec with this pattern. Define a local tactic:

Ltac notin_sub H :=
  match type of H with

Actually, a cleaner way: prove the inversions by strong induction on the multi derivation using the `multi_inv_step` lemma:
```
Lemma multi_step_inv : forall t u, t -->* u -> t = u \/ exists t', step t t' /\ t' -->* u.
Proof. intros t u H. induction H as [ t | a b c Hst Hm IH]. - left; reflexivity. - right; exists b; split; [exact Hst | exact Hm]. Qed.
```

```
Lemma multi_app_inv : forall (f a : tm) (r : label) (v : tm),
  tm_app f a r -->* v -> value v ->
  exists (T : ty) (b av : tm) (k : security),
    f -->* tm_abs T b k /\ value (tm_abs T b k) /\
    a -->* av /\ value av /\ flows_to k.(reader) r /\
    tm_protect k.(indirect_reader) (open b av) -->* v.
Proof.
  intros f a r v H Hv.
  remember (tm_app f a r) as s eqn:Es.
  revert f a r Es.
  induction H as [ s0 | s1 s2 s3 Hst Hm IH ]; intros f0 a0 r0 Es.

```

```
Lemma multi_app_inv : forall f a r v, tm_app f a r -->* v -> value v -> exists ... .
Proof.
  intros f a r v H Hv.
  remember (tm_app f a r) as s eqn:Es.
  revert f a r Es.
  induction H as [ s0 | s1 s2 s3 Hst Hm IH ]; intros f0 a0 r0 Es.
  - subst s0. inversion Hv.
  - subst s1.
    destruct (step_app_dec f0 a0 r0 s2 Hst) as
      [ (T & b & k & av & Hf & Hvabs & Hvav & Ha & Hflow & Hs2)
      | [ (f' & Hstep & Hs2) | (a' & Hvf & Hstep & Hs2) ] ].

```

V definition:
```
Fixpoint V (l : label) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa =>
      flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l ->
      ((exists (w1 w2 : tm) (k1 k2 : security),
          v1 = tm_inl w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl w2 k2 /\
          security_le k1 kappa /\ security_le k2 kappa /\ V l T1 w1 w2)
       \/ (exists (w1 w2 : tm) (k1 k2 : security),
          v1 = tm_inr w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inr w2 k2 /\
          security_le k1 kappa /\ security_le k2 kappa /\ V l T2 w1 w2))
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 kappa =>
      flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l ->
      (exists (w1 w2 w1' w2' : tm) (k1 k2 : security),
          v1 = tm_pair w1 w2 k1 /\ v2 = tm_pair w1' w2' k2 /\
          security_le k1 kappa /\ security_le k2 kappa /\
          V l T1 w1 w1' /\ V l T2 w2 w2')
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa =>
      flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l ->
      match v1, v2 with
      | tm_abs _ b1 k1, tm_abs _ b2 k2 =>
          security_le k1 kappa /\ security_le k2 kappa /\
          (forall (w1 w2 : tm), value w1 -> value w2 -> V l T1 w1 w2 ->
             forall z1 z2, evaluates (open b1 w1) z1 -> evaluates (open b2 w2) z2 ->
               V l T2 z1 z2)
      | _, _ => True
      end
  end.
```

Lemma V_subtype : forall (T U : ty), subtype T U ->
  forall (l : label) (v1 v2 : tm), V l T v1 v2 -> V l U v1 v2.

    | T1 T2 U1 U2 k1 k2 Hsub1 IH1 Hsub2 IH2 Hwf1 Hwf2 Hsle
    | T1 T2 U1 U2 k1 k2 Hsub1 IH1 Hsub2 IH2 Hwf1 Hwf2 Hsle

    intros l v1 v2 HV.

```
Lemma fundamental : forall (Gamma : context) (t : tm) (T : ty),
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall (l : label) (s1 s2 : atom -> tm),
  env_rel l Gamma s1 s2 ->

```
Definition env_rel (l : label) (Gamma : context) (s1 s2 : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  (forall y, locally_closed (s1 y) /\ locally_closed (s2 y)) /\
  (forall x T, lookup_context x Gamma = Some T -> R l T (s1 x) (s2 x)).

Four combinations:
1. EV1 left (inl w1 kr1) and EV2 left: apply HVsum hflow gives disjunction. But the disjunction may be left (inl) or right (inr). Since the actual values are inl, the right branch is impossible by constructor discrimination. We can destruct the disjunction:

3. right/left similar.
4. right/right: use T2 body.

This requires handling contradictions via `discriminate` or `inversion`. The disjunction from HVsum is between existentials of equations; destructing and using `inversion`/`discriminate` on the equation with mismatched constructors should close.

```

For T_Abs, writing the env relation proofs inline might be verbose. Let me create the helper `env_rel_update` and also `env_rel_lc` access.

```
Definition env_rel (l : label) (Gamma : context) (s1 s2 : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  (forall y, locally_closed (s1 y) /\ locally_closed (s2 y)) /\
  (forall x T, lookup_context x Gamma = Some T -> R l T (s1 x) (s2 x)).
```

`env_rel_update`:
```
Lemma env_rel_update : forall (l : label) (Gamma : context)
    (s1 s2 : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (T : ty) (w1 w2 : tm),
  env_rel l Gamma s1 s2 ->
  value w1 -> value w2 -> V l T w1 w2 ->
  env_rel l (update Gamma x T)
    (fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then w1 else s1 y)
    (fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then w2 else s2 y).
Proof.
  intros l Gamma s1 s2 x T w1 w2 [Hlc HR] Hw1 Hw2 HV. split.
  - intros y. destruct (Nat.eqb y x) eqn:E.
    + split; apply value_lc; assumption.
    + apply Hlc.
  - intros y U Hy. simpl in Hy.
    destruct (Nat.eqb y x) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst y. inversion Hy. subst U.
      apply R_of_V_value; assumption.
    + apply HR. exact Hy.
Qed.
```
Hmm, one issue: env_rel's lc is `forall y, lc(s1 y) /\ lc(s2 y)` — I wrote Hlc : forall y, ... ✓.
In the lookup case, `inversion Hy` where Hy : Some T = Some U after simplification? Hy : lookup_context y (update Gamma x T) = Some U. After simpl in Hy, it's `(if y =? x then Some T else lookup_context y Gamma) = Some U`. Destruct eqb true: Hy : Some T = Some U. inversion Hy; subst U ✓. false: Hy : lookup_context y Gamma = Some U; apply HR ✓.

Also `env_rel_weaken`? Not needed.

```
Wait multi_stuck returns u = t, i.e., v1 = tm_abs ...; so no symmetry needed: `apply (multi_stuck ... Ev1)` has type v1 = tm_abs..., matching E1. ✓.
```

    rewrite E1, E2. clear E1 E2 Hval1 Hval2 Ev1 Ev2.
    simpl.
    intro hflow. split.
    + apply security_le_refl.
    + split.
      * apply security_le_refl.
      * intros w1 w2 Hw1 Hw2 HVw z1 z2 [Ez1 Hz1] [Ez2 Hz2].
        set (x := fresh (L ++ fv body)).
        assert (HxL : ~ In x L) by (intro Hin; apply (fresh_notin (L ++ fv body)); apply in_or_app; left; exact Hin).
        assert (Hxbody : ~ In x (fv body)) by (intro Hin; apply (fresh_notin (L ++ fv body)); apply in_or_app; right; exact Hin).
        pose proof (IHbody x HxL l
           (fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then w1 else s1 y)
           (fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then w2 else s2 y)
           (env_rel_update l Gamma s1 s2 x T1 w1 w2 (conj Hlc HR) Hw1 Hw2 HVw)) as HRbody.
        rewrite (subst_env_open_rec s1 x w1 body 0) in HRbody.
        2: { intros y Hy; exact (proj1 (Hlc y)). }
        2: { exact Hxbody. }
        rewrite (subst_env_open_rec s2 x w2 body 0) in HRbody.
        2: { intros y Hy; exact (proj2 (Hlc y)). }
        2: { exact Hxbody. }
        exact (HRbody z1 z2 (conj Ez1 Hz1) (conj Ez2 Hz2)).
```
Wait, subst_env_open_rec's first arg is s, then x, w, body, k. Its conclusion:
subst_env (fun y => if y =? x then w else s y) (open_rec k (fvar x) body) = open_rec k w (subst_env s body).
With k=0, that's subst_env sigma' (open body (fvar x)) = open_rec 0 w (subst_env s body) = open (subst_env s body) w. HRbody has subst_env sigma' (open body (fvar x)); rewriting with the lemma (left-to-right) replaces it. ✓.
But `rewrite ... in HRbody` with side conditions: the lemma's hypotheses are (forall y, y<>x -> lc(s y)) and ~In x (fv body). Using `rewrite (subst_env_open_rec s1 x w1 body 0) in HRbody.` then two subgoals: the lc and the freshness. But since the rewrite was in a hypothesis, the side goals appear. The `2:{}` syntax applies to the second goal of the current focus. Hmm, after `rewrite ... in HRbody.` the focus is on the side conditions? Actually for `rewrite ... in H`, the tactic transforms H and leaves the original goal plus premise subgoals. The `2:` selects the second goal. Let me instead use `erewrite`? T_Case and T_If are the most complex. Let me write them with explicit case analysis. Let me define helper lemmas to extract sum disjuncts maybe:

```
Lemma V_sum_inv : forall l T1 T2 kappa v1 v2,
  V l (Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa) v1 v2 ->
  flows_to kappa.ind l ->
  (exists w1 w2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inl w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl w2 k2 /\ security_le k1 kappa /\ security_le k2 kappa /\ V l T1 w1 w2)
  \/ (exists w1 w2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inr w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inr w2 k2 /\ ...).
Proof. intros. simpl in H. exact (H H0). Qed.
```
This is just applying. Might help clarity. Similarly V_prod_inv. But the raw `simpl in HV; specialize (HV hflow); destruct` works.

For T_Case, after getting the scrutinee evaluations and hflow, the main proof per combination:

Combination LL (both side1 and side2 used inl):
- HVsum specialized gives disjunction D. destruct D:
  * inl case: extract payloads w1',w2', annotations kk1,kk2, equations E1: tm_inl w1 kr1 = tm_inl w1' kk1, E2..., S1,S2, HV1.

```

So the new V design:
```
Fixpoint V l T v1 v2 :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa =>
      match v1, v2 with
      | tm_inl w1 k1, tm_inl w2 k2 =>
          security_le k1 kappa /\ security_le k2 kappa /\
          (flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l -> V l T1 w1 w2)
      | tm_inr w1 k1, tm_inr w2 k2 =>
          security_le k1 kappa /\ security_le k2 kappa /\
          (flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l -> V l T2 w1 w2)
      | _, _ => True
      end
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 kappa =>
      match v1, v2 with
      | tm_pair w1 w2 k1, tm_pair w1' w2' k2 =>
          security_le k1 kappa /\ security_le k2 kappa /\
          (flows_to kappa.ind l -> V l T1 w1 w1' /\ V l T2 w2 w2')
      | _, _ => True
      end
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa =>
      match v1, v2 with
      | tm_abs _ b1 k1, tm_abs _ b2 k2 =>
          security_le k1 kappa /\ security_le k2 kappa /\
          (flows_to kappa.ind l -> forall w1 w2, value w1 -> value w2 -> V l T1 w1 w2 ->
             forall z1 z2, evaluates (open b1 w1) z1 -> evaluates (open b2 w2) z2 -> V l T2 z1 z2)
      | _, _ => True
      end
  end.
```
This is a cleaner "value relation" design. It also simplifies V_vacuous: with the new design, V is not vacuous by an implication at the top; vacuity is inside the conditional payload/body relations. Hmm, V_vacuous as stated (if the type's indirect doesn't flow to l then V holds) is no longer true generally: for a Sum whose values are both inl with annotations NOT ≤ kappa, V would require security_le and fail. But in well-typed settings the annotations are ≤ kappa. Our use of V_vacuous: needed for the main theorem at T_secret (R l T_secret t1 t2 for arbitrary terms t1,t2). With the new V, V at T_secret for arbitrary evaluated values is not automatically true: e.g., T_secret = Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa with values inl/inl but annotations not ≤ kappa — can that happen for well-typed terms? The terms t1,t2 have type T_secret, and if they evaluate to values, the values' annotations should satisfy security_le (by typing). But R is applied to arbitrary evaluations; we need to prove R l T_secret t1 t2 from typing information, not just vacuity. Hmm! In the main theorem, t1,t2 : has_type empty T_secret. The V_protect_intro_cond lemma:
```
Lemma V_protect_intro_cond : forall (l : label) (T : ty) (b a1 a2 : label) (v1 v2 : tm),
  (flows_to b l -> flows_to a1 b) ->
  (flows_to b l -> flows_to a2 b) ->
  (flows_to b l -> V l T v1 v2) ->
  V l (ty_protect b T) (protect_value v1 a1) (protect_value v2 a2).
Proof.
  intros l T b a1 a2 v1 v2 H1 H2 HV.
  destruct T as [kT | Ta Tb kT | Ta Tb kT | Ta Tb kT];
    intro hgoal;
    assert (hb : flows_to b l)
      by (eapply flows_to_trans; [apply flows_to_join_r | exact hgoal]);
    apply (V_protect_adjust _ l v1 v2 (HV hb) a1 a2 b (H1 hb) (H2 hb)).
Qed.
```

Lemma V_ground_erase : forall (kappa0 : security) (T : ty),
  ground T -> transparent_at kappa0 T ->
  forall v1 v2, V kappa0.(indirect_reader) T v1 v2 ->
    erase_security v1 = erase_security v2.
```
Induction on T:
- Ty_Unit k: goal erase_security v1 = erase_security v2. Both are values? V at Unit = True; v1,v2 arbitrary terms maybe. erase_security of arbitrary terms may differ! V at Unit is True for ANY v1,v2, not just values of unit type. In the main theorem, v1,v2 are values evaluated from terms; but they could be arbitrary values (e.g., abs?) if a term typed at Unit evaluates to an abs. But typed terms at Unit should evaluate to units, so v1,v2 are tm_unit... and erase gives tm_unit public. However, our V at Unit doesn't encode that. Hmm. In the main theorem, we get V l T_result v1 v2 where v1,v2 are *values* (from evaluates). For T_result = Unit, need erase_security v1 = erase_security v2 for arbitrary values v1,v2? Not true (e.g., v1 = tm_unit secret, v2 = tm_inl (unit) public). But can those be evaluations of terms typed at Ty_Unit? No. So we need type-correctness of evaluated values.

Options: 
(a) Strengthen the main statement: we need erase equality only for values obtained from evaluations of well-typed terms. We could prove a "value typing inversion" lemma: if has_type Gamma t T and t evaluates to v with value v, then for each type constructor, v has the matching outer constructor and annotations compatible. This requires typing preservation through evaluation, which we don't have.
(b) Alternatively, extend V at Unit to require erase equality! V at Unit kappa := erase_security v1 = erase_security v2 (or v1 = tm_unit...?). But V_protect_adjust Unit: need erase(protect_value v1 a1) = erase(protect_value v2 a2) from erase v1 = erase v2: erase_security_protect_value gives erase(protect_value vi ai) = erase vi ✓. V_subtype Unit trivial. V_protect_intro_cond Unit: the lemma's proof does `simpl. exact I` for the unit case, but with V Unit = erase equality, the goal isn't True. Hmm, V_protect_intro_cond's unit case would need to prove erase(protect_value v1 a1) = erase(protect_value v2 a2) from HV hb : V ... = erase v1 = erase v2. V_ground_erase compiled (including the f_equal goals apparently). Now the main theorem proof. Let me write it carefully.

```
Theorem noninterference : forall (C : program_context) (t1 t2 : tm)
    (T_secret T_result : ty),
  has_type empty t1 T_secret ->
  has_type empty t2 T_secret ->
  context_has_type C T_secret T_result ->
  ground T_result ->
  transparent T_result ->
  ~ flows_to (security_of T_secret).(indirect_reader)
      (security_of T_result).(indirect_reader) ->
  same_result (plug C t1) (plug C t2).
Proof.
  intros C t1 t2 T_secret T_result Ht1 Ht2 HC Hg Htr Hnf.
  destruct HC as [L HC].
  set (x0 := fresh (L ++ fv (plug C (tm_fvar 0)))).
  assert (HxL : ~ In x0 L).
  { intro Hin. pose proof (fresh_notin (L ++ fv (plug C (tm_fvar 0)))) as Hni.
    apply Hni. apply in_or_app. left. exact Hin. }
  assert (HxC : ~ In x0 (fv (plug C (tm_fvar 0)))).
  { intro Hin. pose proof (fresh_notin (L ++ fv (plug C (tm_fvar 0)))) as Hni.
    apply Hni. apply in_or_app. right. exact Hin. }
  assert (Hxctx : ~ In x0 (ctx_atoms C)).
  { intro Hin. apply HxC. apply (proj2 (in_fv_plug C 0 x0)). left. exact Hin. }
  assert (Htyp : has_type (update empty x0 T_secret) (plug C (tm_fvar x0)) T_result).
  { apply HC. exact HxL. }
  assert (Hlc1 : locally_closed t1) by (apply has_type_lc; exact Ht1).
  assert (Hlc2 : locally_closed t2) by (apply has_type_lc; exact Ht2).
  set (s1 := fun y => if Nat.eqb y x0 then t1 else tm_fvar y).
  set (s2 := fun y => if Nat.eqb y x0 then t2 else tm_fvar y).
  assert (Henv : env_rel (security_of T_result).(indirect_reader)
                   (update empty x0 T_secret) s1 s2).
  { split.
    - intros y. unfold s1, s2. destruct (Nat.eqb y x0) eqn:E.
      + split; assumption.
      + split; simpl; exact I.
    - intros y T Hy. simpl in Hy.
      destruct (Nat.eqb y x0) eqn:E.
      + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst y. inversion Hy. subst T.
        unfold R. intros v1 v2 _ _.
        unfold s1, s2. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. simpl.
        apply V_vacuous. exact Hnf.
      + discriminate Hy.
  }
  pose proof (fundamental (update empty x0 T_secret) (plug C (tm_fvar x0)) T_result Htyp
                (security_of T_result).(indirect_reader) s1 s2 Henv) as HR.
  assert (Hs1 : subst_env s1 (plug C (tm_fvar x0)) = plug C t1).
  { unfold s1. apply (subst_env_plug_hole C x0 t1 Hxctx). }
  assert (Hs2 : subst_env s2 (plug C (tm_fvar x0)) = plug C t2).
  { unfold s2. apply (subst_env_plug_hole C x0 t2 Hxctx). }
  rewrite Hs1, Hs2 in HR.
  unfold same_result. intros v1 v2 Ev1 Ev2.
  apply (V_ground_erase T_result (security_of T_result) Hg Htr v1 v2).
  apply HR; assumption.
Qed.
```
